    window.loadScript = (url, { charset = 'UTF-8', ...attributes } = {}, location = 'head') => {
      const existingScript = document.querySelector('script[src="' + url + '"]');
      if (existingScript) {
        return;
      }

      const script = document.createElement('script');
      script.src = url;
      script.type = 'text/javascript';

      script.async = true;
      script.charset = charset;

      if (attributes) {
        Object.entries(attributes).forEach(([key, value]) => {
          script.setAttribute(key, value);
        });
      }

      if (location === 'head') {
        document.head.appendChild(script);
      } else {
        document.body.appendChild(script);
      }

      return script;
    };

security&quot;],&quot;canonical&quot;:[0,&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/post-quantum-roadmap&quot;],&quot;info&quot;:[0,{&quot;id&quot;:[0,&quot;1vlCZshPEwWazkAnJsxN44&quot;],&quot;title&quot;:[0,&quot;Cloudflare targets 2029 for full post-quantum security&quot;],&quot;slug&quot;:[0,&quot;post-quantum-roadmap&quot;],&quot;excerpt&quot;:[0,&quot;Recent advances in quantum hardware and software have accelerated the timeline on which quantum attack might happen. Cloudflare is responding by moving our target for full post-quantum security to 2029.&quot;],&quot;featured&quot;:[0,false],&quot;html&quot;:[0,&quot;&lt;p&gt;Cloudflare is accelerating its post-quantum roadmap. We now target &lt;b&gt;2029&lt;/b&gt; to be fully post-quantum (PQ) secure including, crucially, post-quantum authentication.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;At Cloudflare, we believe in making the Internet private and secure by default. We started by offering &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/introducing-universal-ssl/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;free universal SSL certificates&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; in 2014, began preparing our &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/towards-post-quantum-cryptography-in-tls/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;post-quantum migration&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; in 2019, and enabled post-quantum encryption for &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/post-quantum-for-all/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;all websites&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; and APIs in 2022, mitigating harvest-now/decrypt-later attacks. While weâre excited by the fact that over &lt;a href=\&quot;https://radar.cloudflare.com/post-quantum\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;65% of human traffic&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; to Cloudflare is post-quantum encrypted, our work is not done until authentication is also upgraded. Credible new research and rapid industry developments suggest that the deadline to migrate is much sooner than expected. This is a challenge that any organization must treat with urgency, which is why weâre expediting our own internal Q-Day readiness timeline.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;What happened? Last week, Google &lt;a href=\&quot;https://research.google/blog/safeguarding-cryptocurrency-by-disclosing-quantum-vulnerabilities-responsibly/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;announced&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; they had drastically improved upon the quantum algorithm to break elliptic curve cryptography, which is widely used to secure the Internet. They did not reveal the algorithm, but instead provided a &lt;a href=\&quot;https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zero-knowledge_proof\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;zero-knowledge proof&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; that they have one.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;This is not even the biggest breakthrough. That same day, Oratomic &lt;a href=\&quot;https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.28627\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;published&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; a resource estimate for breaking RSA-2048 and P-256 on a neutral atom computer. For P-256, it only requires a shockingly low 10,000 qubits. Googleâs motivation behind their recent announcement to also pursue &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.google/innovation-and-ai/technology/research/neutral-atom-quantum-computers/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;neutral atoms&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; alongside superconducting quantum computers becomes clear now. Although Oratomic explains their basic approach, they still leave out crucial details &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.oratomic.com/news/responsible-disclosure-in-the-era-of-quantum-computers\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;on purpose&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;These independent advances prompted Google to accelerate their post-quantum migration timeline to &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.google/innovation-and-ai/technology/safety-security/cryptography-migration-timeline/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;2029&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. Whatâs more, in their announcement and &lt;a href=\&quot;https://westerbaan.name/~bas/rwpqc2026/sophie.pdf\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;other talks&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, Google has placed a priority on quantum-secure authentication over mitigating harvest-now/decrypt-later attacks. As we discuss next, this priority indicates that Google is concerned about Q-Day coming as soon as 2030. Following the announcements, IBM Quantum Safeâs CTO is more pessimistic and canât rule out quantum âmoonshot attacksâ on high value targets &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.linkedin.com/pulse/quantum-timeline-elliptic-curve-cryptography-just-jumped-osborne-k1oae\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;as early as 2029&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;The quantum threat is well known: Q-Day is the day that sufficiently capable quantum computers can break essential cryptography used to protect data and access across systems today.&lt;i&gt; &lt;/i&gt;Cryptographically relevant quantum computers (CRQCs) donât exist yet, but many labs across the world are pursuing different approaches to building one. Until recently, progress on CRQCs has been mostly public, but there is no reason to expect that will continue. Indeed, there is ample reason to expect that progress will leave the public eye. As quantum computer scientist Scott Aaronson &lt;a href=\&quot;https://scottaaronson.blog/?p=9425\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;warned&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; at the end of 2025:&lt;/p&gt;&lt;blockquote&gt;&lt;p&gt;[A]t some point, the people doing detailed estimates of how many physical qubits and gates itâll take to break actually deployed cryptosystems using Shorâs algorithm are going to stop publishing those estimates, if for no other reason than the risk of giving too much information to adversaries. Indeed, for all we know, that point may have been passed already.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/blockquote&gt;&lt;p&gt;That point has now passed indeed.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;why-now-independent-progress-on-three-fronts\&quot;&gt;Why now: independent progress on three fronts&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#why-now-independent-progress-on-three-fronts\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;Weâd like to spend some words on why itâs difficult to predict progress on quantum computing. Sudden âquantumâ leaps in understanding, like the one we witnessed last week, can occur even if everything happens in the public eye. Simply put, breaking cryptography with a quantum computer requires engineering on three independent fronts: quantum hardware, error correction, and quantum software. Progress on each front compounds progress on the others.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Hardware.&lt;/b&gt; There are many different competing approaches. We mentioned neutral atoms and superconducting qubits, but there are also ion-trap, photonics, and moonshots like topological qubits. Complementary approaches can even be &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.caltech.edu/about/news/low-noise-transducers-to-bridge-the-gap-between-microwave-and-optical-qubits\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;combined&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. Most of these approaches are pursued by several labs around the world. They all have their distinct engineering challenges and problems to solve before they can scale up. A few years ago, all of them had a long list of open challenges, and it was unclear if any of them would scale. Today most of them have made good progress. None have been demonstrated to scale yet: if they had, we wouldnât have a couple of years left. But these approaches are much closer now, especially neutral atoms. To ignore this progress, youâd have to believe that every single approach will hit a wall.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Error correction.&lt;/b&gt; All quantum computers are noisy and require error-correcting codes to perform meaningful computation. This adds quite a bit of overhead, though how much depends on the architecture. More noise requires more error correction, but more interestingly, improved qubit connectivity allows for much more efficient codes. For a sense of scale: typically around a thousand physical qubits are required for one logical qubit for the superconducting quantum computers that are noisy and only have neighbor qubit connectivity. We knew âreconfigurable qubitsâ such as those of neutral-atom machines allow for an order of magnitude better error-correcting codes. Surprisingly, Oratomic showed the advantage is even larger: only about 3-4 physical neutral atom qubits are required per logical qubit.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Software. &lt;/b&gt;Lastly, the quantum algorithms to crack cryptography can be improved. This is Googleâs breakthrough: they massively sped up the algorithm to crack P-256. On top of that, Oratomic showed further architecture specific optimizations for reconfigurable qubits.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;The picture comes together: in 2025 neutral atoms turned out to be more scalable than expected, and now Oratomic figured out how to do much better error-correcting codes with such highly connected qubits. On top of that, breaking P-256 requires much less work. The result is that Q-Day has been pulled forward significantly from typical &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/pq-2025/#is-q-day-always-fifteen-years-away\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;2035+ timelines&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, with neutral atoms in the lead, and other approaches not far behind.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;In previous blog posts &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/pq-2025/#progress-on-quantum-hardware\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;weâve discussed&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; how different quantum computers compare on physical qubit count and fidelity, compared to the conservative goalpost of cracking RSA-2048 on a superconducting qubit architecture. This analysis gives us a rough idea of how much time we have, and itâs certainly better than tracking &lt;a href=\&quot;https://bas.westerbaan.name/notes/2026/04/02/factoring.html\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;quantum factoring records&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, but it misses architecture-specific optimization and software improvements. What to watch for now is when the final missing &lt;a href=\&quot;https://westerbaan.name/~bas/rwpqc2026/adam.pdf\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;capabilities&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; for each architecture are achieved.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;its-time-to-focus-on-authentication\&quot;&gt;Itâs time to focus on authentication&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#its-time-to-focus-on-authentication\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;Historically, the industryâs focus on post-quantum cryptography (PQC) has been based largely on PQ &lt;i&gt;encryption, &lt;/i&gt;which stops harvest-now/decrypt-later (HNDL) attacks. In an HNDL attack, an adversary harvests sensitive encrypted network traffic today and stores it until a future date when it can use a powerful quantum computer to decrypt the data. HNDL attacks are the primary threat when Q-Day is far away. Thatâs why our focus, thus far, has been on mitigating this risk, by adopting post-quantum encryption by default in our products &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/post-quantum-for-all/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;since 2022&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. Today, as we mentioned above, &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/ssl/post-quantum-cryptography/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;most Cloudflare products&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; are secure against HNDL attacks, and weâre working to upgrade the rest as we speak.Â &lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;The other category of attacks is against authentication: adversaries armed with functioning quantum computers impersonate servers or forge access credentials. If Q-Day is far off, authentication is not urgent: deploying PQ certificates and signatures does not add any value, only effort.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;An imminent Q-Day flips the script: data leaks are severe, but broken authentication is catastrophic. Any overlooked quantum-vulnerable remote-login key is an access point for an attacker to do as they wish, whether thatâs to extort, take down, or snoop on your system. Any automatic software-update mechanism becomes a &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.cloudflare.com/learning/security/what-is-remote-code-execution/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;remote code execution&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; vector. An active quantum attacker has it easy â they only need to find one trusted quantum-vulnerable key to get in.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;When experts in the field of building quantum computers start patching authentication systems, we should all listen. The question is no longer &amp;quot;when will our encrypted data be at risk?&amp;quot; but &amp;quot;how long before an attacker walks in the front door with a quantum-forged key?&amp;quot;&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;prioritizing-the-most-vulnerable-systems\&quot;&gt;Prioritizing the most vulnerable systems&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#prioritizing-the-most-vulnerable-systems\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;If quantum computers arrive in the next few years, they will be scarce and expensive. Attackers will prioritize high-value targets, like long-lived keys that unlock substantial assets or persistent access such as root certificates, API auth keys and code-signing certs. If an attacker is able to compromise one such key, they retain indefinite access until they are discovered or that key is revoked.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;This suggests long-lived keys should be prioritized. That is certainly true if the quantum attack of a single key is expensive and slow, which is to be expected for the first generation of neutral atom quantum computers. Thatâs not the case for scalable superconducting quantum computers and later generations of neutral atom quantum computers, which could well crack keys much faster. Such fast CRQCs flip the script again, and an adversary with one might focus purely on HNDL attacks so that their attacks remain undetected. Googleâs Sophie Schmieg &lt;a href=\&quot;https://westerbaan.name/~bas/rwpqc2026/sophie.pdf\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;compares&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; this scenario to Enigmaâs cryptanalysis that changed the direction of World War II.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Adding support for PQ cryptography is not enough. Systems must disable support for quantum-vulnerable cryptography to be secure against &lt;a href=\&quot;https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Downgrade_attack\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;downgrade attacks&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. In larger, especially federated systems such as the web, this is not feasible because not every client (browser) will support post-quantum certificates, and servers need to keep supporting these legacy clients. However, downgrade protection for HTTPS is still achievable using â&lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.chromium.org/Home/chromium-security/post-quantum-auth-roadmap\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;PQ HSTS&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;â and/or &lt;a href=\&quot;https://westerbaan.name/~bas/rwpqc2026/bas.pdf\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;certificate transparency&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Disabling quantum-vulnerable cryptography is not the last step: once done, all secrets such as passwords and access tokens previously exposed in the quantum-vulnerable system need to be rotated. Unlike post-quantum encryption, which takes one big push, migrating to post-quantum authentication has a long dependency chain â not to mention third-party validation and fraud monitoring. This will take years, not months.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Itâs natural for organizations reading this to rush out and think about which internal systems they need to upgrade. But thatâs not the end of the story. Q-day threatens all systems. As such, itâs important to understand the impact of a potential Q-day on third-party dependencies, both direct and indirect. Not just the third-parties you speak cryptography to, but also any third parties that are critical business dependencies like financial services and utilities.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;With Q-day approaching on a shorter timeline, post-quantum authentication is top priority. Long-term keys should be upgraded first. Deep dependency chains and the fact that everyone has third-party vendors means this effort will take on the order of years, not months. Upgrading to post-quantum cryptography is not enough: to prevent downgrades, quantum-vulnerable cryptography must also be turned off.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;cloudflares-roadmap-to-full-post-quantum-security\&quot;&gt;Cloudflareâs roadmap to full post-quantum security&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#cloudflares-roadmap-to-full-post-quantum-security\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;Today, Cloudflare provides post-quantum encryption for the majority of our products mitigating harvest-now/decrypt-later. This is the product of work we started &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/introducing-universal-ssl/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;over a decade ago&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; to protect our customers and the Internet at large.\n\nWe are targeting full post-quantum security including authentication for our entire product suite by 2029. Here weâre sharing some intermediate milestones weâve set, subject to change as our understanding of the risk and deployment challenges evolve.&lt;/p&gt;\n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/CxLrmxb2dExy2S5FL0ouy/e1ee5616ce31d15ed8e0fd7b40ae2a39/image2.jpg\&quot; alt=\&quot;Mid-2026: PQ authentication (ML-DSA) support for Cloudflare â origin connections. Mid-2027: PQ authentication support for visitor â Cloudflare connections using Merkle Tree Certificates. Early 2028: Cloudflare One SASE suite adds PQ authentication to make it fully PQ secure. 2029: Cloudflare fully post-quantum secure.\&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;1520\&quot; height=\&quot;850\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n          &lt;/figure&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;what-we-recommend\&quot;&gt;What we recommend&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#what-we-recommend\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;For businesses, we recommend making post-quantum support a requirement for any procurement. Common best practices, like keeping software updated and automating certificate issuance, are meaningful and will get you pretty far. We recommend assessing critical vendors early for what their failure to take action would mean for your business.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;For regulatory agencies and governments: leading by setting early timelines has been crucial for industry-wide progress so far. We are now in a pivotal position where fragmentation in standards and effort between and within jurisdictions could put progress at risk. We recommend that governments assign and empower a lead agency to coordinate the migration on a clear timeline, stay security-focused, and promote the use of existing international standards. Governments need not panic, but can lead migration with confidence.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;For Cloudflare customers, with respect to our services, you do not need to take any mitigating action. We are following the latest advancements in quantum computing closely and taking proactive steps to protect your data. As we have done in the past, we will turn on post-quantum security by default, with no switches to flip. What we donât control is the other side: browsers, applications, and origins need to upgrade. Corporate network traffic on Cloudflare need not worry: Cloudflare One offers &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/post-quantum-sase/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;end-to-end protection&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; when tunnelling traffic through our post-quantum encrypted infrastructure.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Privacy and security are table stakes for the Internet. That&amp;#39;s why every post-quantum upgrade we build will continue to be available to all customers, on every plan, at &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/post-quantum-crypto-should-be-free/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;no additional cost&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. Making post-quantum security the default is the only way to protect the Internet at scale.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/introducing-universal-ssl/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Free TLS&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; helped encrypt the web. Free post-quantum cryptography will help secure it for what comes next.&lt;/p&gt;&quot;],&quot;published_at&quot;:[0,&quot;2026-04-07T22:00+01:00&quot;],&quot;updated_at&quot;:[0,&quot;2026-04-07T13:58:14.020Z&quot;],&quot;feature_image&quot;:[0,&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/6siStuS5VWsIdEag8FQZkF/2e014e951328beb480468dffda783c2d/image1.png&quot;],&quot;tags&quot;:[1,[[0,{&quot;id&quot;:[0,&quot;6bIo7ayy56Fzdrtf9z2EWy&quot;],&quot;name&quot;:[0,&quot;Post-Quantum&quot;],&quot;slug&quot;:[0,&quot;post-quantum&quot;]}],[0,{&quot;id&quot;:[0,&quot;6Mp7ouACN2rT3YjL1xaXJx&quot;],&quot;name&quot;:[0,&quot;Security&quot;],&quot;slug&quot;:[0,&quot;security&quot;]}]]],&quot;relatedTags&quot;:[0],&quot;authors&quot;:[1,[[0,{&quot;name&quot;:[0,&quot;Bas Westerbaan&quot;],&quot;slug&quot;:[0,&quot;bas&quot;],&quot;bio&quot;:[0,null],&quot;profile_image&quot;:[0,&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/4KeBG8XO1ADZHuEV9v5Hwz/6c90a96cfb1373b208bffc35f3fd71f4/bas.png&quot;],&quot;location&quot;:[0,&quot;The Netherlands&quot;],&quot;website&quot;:[0,&quot;https://bas.westerbaan.name&quot;],&quot;twitter&quot;:[0,&quot;@bwesterb&quot;],&quot;facebook&quot;:[0,null],&quot;publiclyIndex&quot;:[0,true]}]]],&quot;meta_description&quot;:[0,&quot;Recent advances in quantum hardware and software have accelerated the timeline on which quantum attack might happen. props="{&quot;post&quot;:[0,{&quot;id&quot;:[0,&quot;1vlCZshPEwWazkAnJsxN44&quot;],&quot;title&quot;:[0,&quot;Cloudflare targets 2029 for full post-quantum security&quot;],&quot;slug&quot;:[0,&quot;post-quantum-roadmap&quot;],&quot;excerpt&quot;:[0,&quot;Recent advances in quantum hardware and software have accelerated the timeline on which quantum attack might happen. Cloudflare is responding by moving our target for full post-quantum security to 2029.&quot;],&quot;featured&quot;:[0,false],&quot;html&quot;:[0,&quot;&lt;p&gt;Cloudflare is accelerating its post-quantum roadmap. We now target &lt;b&gt;2029&lt;/b&gt; to be fully post-quantum (PQ) secure including, crucially, post-quantum authentication.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;At Cloudflare, we believe in making the Internet private and secure by default. We started by offering &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/introducing-universal-ssl/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;free universal SSL certificates&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; in 2014, began preparing our &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/towards-post-quantum-cryptography-in-tls/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;post-quantum migration&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; in 2019, and enabled post-quantum encryption for &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/post-quantum-for-all/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;all websites&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; and APIs in 2022, mitigating harvest-now/decrypt-later attacks. While weâre excited by the fact that over &lt;a href=\&quot;https://radar.cloudflare.com/post-quantum\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;65% of human traffic&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; to Cloudflare is post-quantum encrypted, our work is not done until authentication is also upgraded. Credible new research and rapid industry developments suggest that the deadline to migrate is much sooner than expected. This is a challenge that any organization must treat with urgency, which is why weâre expediting our own internal Q-Day readiness timeline.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;What happened? Last week, Google &lt;a href=\&quot;https://research.google/blog/safeguarding-cryptocurrency-by-disclosing-quantum-vulnerabilities-responsibly/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;announced&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; they had drastically improved upon the quantum algorithm to break elliptic curve cryptography, which is widely used to secure the Internet. They did not reveal the algorithm, but instead provided a &lt;a href=\&quot;https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zero-knowledge_proof\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;zero-knowledge proof&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; that they have one.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;This is not even the biggest breakthrough. That same day, Oratomic &lt;a href=\&quot;https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.28627\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;published&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; a resource estimate for breaking RSA-2048 and P-256 on a neutral atom computer. For P-256, it only requires a shockingly low 10,000 qubits. Googleâs motivation behind their recent announcement to also pursue &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.google/innovation-and-ai/technology/research/neutral-atom-quantum-computers/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;neutral atoms&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; alongside superconducting quantum computers becomes clear now. Although Oratomic explains their basic approach, they still leave out crucial details &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.oratomic.com/news/responsible-disclosure-in-the-era-of-quantum-computers\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;on purpose&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;These independent advances prompted Google to accelerate their post-quantum migration timeline to &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.google/innovation-and-ai/technology/safety-security/cryptography-migration-timeline/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;2029&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. Whatâs more, in their announcement and &lt;a href=\&quot;https://westerbaan.name/~bas/rwpqc2026/sophie.pdf\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;other talks&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, Google has placed a priority on quantum-secure authentication over mitigating harvest-now/decrypt-later attacks. As we discuss next, this priority indicates that Google is concerned about Q-Day coming as soon as 2030. Following the announcements, IBM Quantum Safeâs CTO is more pessimistic and canât rule out quantum âmoonshot attacksâ on high value targets &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.linkedin.com/pulse/quantum-timeline-elliptic-curve-cryptography-just-jumped-osborne-k1oae\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;as early as 2029&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;The quantum threat is well known: Q-Day is the day that sufficiently capable quantum computers can break essential cryptography used to protect data and access across systems today.&lt;i&gt; &lt;/i&gt;Cryptographically relevant quantum computers (CRQCs) donât exist yet, but many labs across the world are pursuing different approaches to building one. Until recently, progress on CRQCs has been mostly public, but there is no reason to expect that will continue. Indeed, there is ample reason to expect that progress will leave the public eye. As quantum computer scientist Scott Aaronson &lt;a href=\&quot;https://scottaaronson.blog/?p=9425\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;warned&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; at the end of 2025:&lt;/p&gt;&lt;blockquote&gt;&lt;p&gt;[A]t some point, the people doing detailed estimates of how many physical qubits and gates itâll take to break actually deployed cryptosystems using Shorâs algorithm are going to stop publishing those estimates, if for no other reason than the risk of giving too much information to adversaries. Indeed, for all we know, that point may have been passed already.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/blockquote&gt;&lt;p&gt;That point has now passed indeed.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;why-now-independent-progress-on-three-fronts\&quot;&gt;Why now: independent progress on three fronts&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#why-now-independent-progress-on-three-fronts\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;Weâd like to spend some words on why itâs difficult to predict progress on quantum computing. Sudden âquantumâ leaps in understanding, like the one we witnessed last week, can occur even if everything happens in the public eye. Simply put, breaking cryptography with a quantum computer requires engineering on three independent fronts: quantum hardware, error correction, and quantum software. Progress on each front compounds progress on the others.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Hardware.&lt;/b&gt; There are many different competing approaches. We mentioned neutral atoms and superconducting qubits, but there are also ion-trap, photonics, and moonshots like topological qubits. Complementary approaches can even be &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.caltech.edu/about/news/low-noise-transducers-to-bridge-the-gap-between-microwave-and-optical-qubits\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;combined&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. Most of these approaches are pursued by several labs around the world. They all have their distinct engineering challenges and problems to solve before they can scale up. A few years ago, all of them had a long list of open challenges, and it was unclear if any of them would scale. Today most of them have made good progress. None have been demonstrated to scale yet: if they had, we wouldnât have a couple of years left. But these approaches are much closer now, especially neutral atoms. To ignore this progress, youâd have to believe that every single approach will hit a wall.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Error correction.&lt;/b&gt; All quantum computers are noisy and require error-correcting codes to perform meaningful computation. This adds quite a bit of overhead, though how much depends on the architecture. More noise requires more error correction, but more interestingly, improved qubit connectivity allows for much more efficient codes. For a sense of scale: typically around a thousand physical qubits are required for one logical qubit for the superconducting quantum computers that are noisy and only have neighbor qubit connectivity. We knew âreconfigurable qubitsâ such as those of neutral-atom machines allow for an order of magnitude better error-correcting codes. Surprisingly, Oratomic showed the advantage is even larger: only about 3-4 physical neutral atom qubits are required per logical qubit.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Software. &lt;/b&gt;Lastly, the quantum algorithms to crack cryptography can be improved. This is Googleâs breakthrough: they massively sped up the algorithm to crack P-256. On top of that, Oratomic showed further architecture specific optimizations for reconfigurable qubits.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;The picture comes together: in 2025 neutral atoms turned out to be more scalable than expected, and now Oratomic figured out how to do much better error-correcting codes with such highly connected qubits. On top of that, breaking P-256 requires much less work. The result is that Q-Day has been pulled forward significantly from typical &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/pq-2025/#is-q-day-always-fifteen-years-away\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;2035+ timelines&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, with neutral atoms in the lead, and other approaches not far behind.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;In previous blog posts &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/pq-2025/#progress-on-quantum-hardware\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;weâve discussed&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; how different quantum computers compare on physical qubit count and fidelity, compared to the conservative goalpost of cracking RSA-2048 on a superconducting qubit architecture. This analysis gives us a rough idea of how much time we have, and itâs certainly better than tracking &lt;a href=\&quot;https://bas.westerbaan.name/notes/2026/04/02/factoring.html\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;quantum factoring records&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, but it misses architecture-specific optimization and software improvements. What to watch for now is when the final missing &lt;a href=\&quot;https://westerbaan.name/~bas/rwpqc2026/adam.pdf\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;capabilities&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; for each architecture are achieved.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;its-time-to-focus-on-authentication\&quot;&gt;Itâs time to focus on authentication&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#its-time-to-focus-on-authentication\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;Historically, the industryâs focus on post-quantum cryptography (PQC) has been based largely on PQ &lt;i&gt;encryption, &lt;/i&gt;which stops harvest-now/decrypt-later (HNDL) attacks. In an HNDL attack, an adversary harvests sensitive encrypted network traffic today and stores it until a future date when it can use a powerful quantum computer to decrypt the data. HNDL attacks are the primary threat when Q-Day is far away. Thatâs why our focus, thus far, has been on mitigating this risk, by adopting post-quantum encryption by default in our products &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/post-quantum-for-all/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;since 2022&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. Today, as we mentioned above, &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/ssl/post-quantum-cryptography/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;most Cloudflare products&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; are secure against HNDL attacks, and weâre working to upgrade the rest as we speak.Â &lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;The other category of attacks is against authentication: adversaries armed with functioning quantum computers impersonate servers or forge access credentials. If Q-Day is far off, authentication is not urgent: deploying PQ certificates and signatures does not add any value, only effort.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;An imminent Q-Day flips the script: data leaks are severe, but broken authentication is catastrophic. Any overlooked quantum-vulnerable remote-login key is an access point for an attacker to do as they wish, whether thatâs to extort, take down, or snoop on your system. Any automatic software-update mechanism becomes a &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.cloudflare.com/learning/security/what-is-remote-code-execution/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;remote code execution&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; vector. An active quantum attacker has it easy â they only need to find one trusted quantum-vulnerable key to get in.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;When experts in the field of building quantum computers start patching authentication systems, we should all listen. The question is no longer &amp;quot;when will our encrypted data be at risk?&amp;quot; but &amp;quot;how long before an attacker walks in the front door with a quantum-forged key?&amp;quot;&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;prioritizing-the-most-vulnerable-systems\&quot;&gt;Prioritizing the most vulnerable systems&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#prioritizing-the-most-vulnerable-systems\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;If quantum computers arrive in the next few years, they will be scarce and expensive. Attackers will prioritize high-value targets, like long-lived keys that unlock substantial assets or persistent access such as root certificates, API auth keys and code-signing certs. If an attacker is able to compromise one such key, they retain indefinite access until they are discovered or that key is revoked.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;This suggests long-lived keys should be prioritized. That is certainly true if the quantum attack of a single key is expensive and slow, which is to be expected for the first generation of neutral atom quantum computers. Thatâs not the case for scalable superconducting quantum computers and later generations of neutral atom quantum computers, which could well crack keys much faster. Such fast CRQCs flip the script again, and an adversary with one might focus purely on HNDL attacks so that their attacks remain undetected. Googleâs Sophie Schmieg &lt;a href=\&quot;https://westerbaan.name/~bas/rwpqc2026/sophie.pdf\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;compares&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; this scenario to Enigmaâs cryptanalysis that changed the direction of World War II.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Adding support for PQ cryptography is not enough. Systems must disable support for quantum-vulnerable cryptography to be secure against &lt;a href=\&quot;https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Downgrade_attack\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;downgrade attacks&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. In larger, especially federated systems such as the web, this is not feasible because not every client (browser) will support post-quantum certificates, and servers need to keep supporting these legacy clients. However, downgrade protection for HTTPS is still achievable using â&lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.chromium.org/Home/chromium-security/post-quantum-auth-roadmap\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;PQ HSTS&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;â and/or &lt;a href=\&quot;https://westerbaan.name/~bas/rwpqc2026/bas.pdf\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;certificate transparency&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Disabling quantum-vulnerable cryptography is not the last step: once done, all secrets such as passwords and access tokens previously exposed in the quantum-vulnerable system need to be rotated. Unlike post-quantum encryption, which takes one big push, migrating to post-quantum authentication has a long dependency chain â not to mention third-party validation and fraud monitoring. This will take years, not months.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Itâs natural for organizations reading this to rush out and think about which internal systems they need to upgrade. But thatâs not the end of the story. Q-day threatens all systems. As such, itâs important to understand the impact of a potential Q-day on third-party dependencies, both direct and indirect. Not just the third-parties you speak cryptography to, but also any third parties that are critical business dependencies like financial services and utilities.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;With Q-day approaching on a shorter timeline, post-quantum authentication is top priority. Long-term keys should be upgraded first. Deep dependency chains and the fact that everyone has third-party vendors means this effort will take on the order of years, not months. Upgrading to post-quantum cryptography is not enough: to prevent downgrades, quantum-vulnerable cryptography must also be turned off.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;cloudflares-roadmap-to-full-post-quantum-security\&quot;&gt;Cloudflareâs roadmap to full post-quantum security&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#cloudflares-roadmap-to-full-post-quantum-security\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;Today, Cloudflare provides post-quantum encryption for the majority of our products mitigating harvest-now/decrypt-later. This is the product of work we started &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/introducing-universal-ssl/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;over a decade ago&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; to protect our customers and the Internet at large.\n\nWe are targeting full post-quantum security including authentication for our entire product suite by 2029. Here weâre sharing some intermediate milestones weâve set, subject to change as our understanding of the risk and deployment challenges evolve.&lt;/p&gt;\n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/CxLrmxb2dExy2S5FL0ouy/e1ee5616ce31d15ed8e0fd7b40ae2a39/image2.jpg\&quot; alt=\&quot;Mid-2026: PQ authentication (ML-DSA) support for Cloudflare â origin connections. Mid-2027: PQ authentication support for visitor â Cloudflare connections using Merkle Tree Certificates. Early 2028: Cloudflare One SASE suite adds PQ authentication to make it fully PQ secure. 2029: Cloudflare fully post-quantum secure.\&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;1520\&quot; height=\&quot;850\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n          &lt;/figure&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;what-we-recommend\&quot;&gt;What we recommend&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#what-we-recommend\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;For businesses, we recommend making post-quantum support a requirement for any procurement. Common best practices, like keeping software updated and automating certificate issuance, are meaningful and will get you pretty far. We recommend assessing critical vendors early for what their failure to take action would mean for your business.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;For regulatory agencies and governments: leading by setting early timelines has been crucial for industry-wide progress so far. We are now in a pivotal position where fragmentation in standards and effort between and within jurisdictions could put progress at risk. We recommend that governments assign and empower a lead agency to coordinate the migration on a clear timeline, stay security-focused, and promote the use of existing international standards. Governments need not panic, but can lead migration with confidence.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;For Cloudflare customers, with respect to our services, you do not need to take any mitigating action. We are following the latest advancements in quantum computing closely and taking proactive steps to protect your data. As we have done in the past, we will turn on post-quantum security by default, with no switches to flip. What we donât control is the other side: browsers, applications, and origins need to upgrade. Corporate network traffic on Cloudflare need not worry: Cloudflare One offers &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/post-quantum-sase/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;end-to-end protection&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; when tunnelling traffic through our post-quantum encrypted infrastructure.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Privacy and security are table stakes for the Internet. That&amp;#39;s why every post-quantum upgrade we build will continue to be available to all customers, on every plan, at &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/post-quantum-crypto-should-be-free/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;no additional cost&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. Making post-quantum security the default is the only way to protect the Internet at scale.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/introducing-universal-ssl/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Free TLS&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; helped encrypt the web. Free post-quantum cryptography will help secure it for what comes next.&lt;/p&gt;&quot;],&quot;published_at&quot;:[0,&quot;2026-04-07T22:00+01:00&quot;],&quot;updated_at&quot;:[0,&quot;2026-04-07T13:58:14.020Z&quot;],&quot;feature_image&quot;:[0,&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/6siStuS5VWsIdEag8FQZkF/2e014e951328beb480468dffda783c2d/image1.png&quot;],&quot;tags&quot;:[1,[[0,{&quot;id&quot;:[0,&quot;6bIo7ayy56Fzdrtf9z2EWy&quot;],&quot;name&quot;:[0,&quot;Post-Quantum&quot;],&quot;slug&quot;:[0,&quot;post-quantum&quot;]}],[0,{&quot;id&quot;:[0,&quot;6Mp7ouACN2rT3YjL1xaXJx&quot;],&quot;name&quot;:[0,&quot;Security&quot;],&quot;slug&quot;:[0,&quot;security&quot;]}]]],&quot;relatedTags&quot;:[0],&quot;authors&quot;:[1,[[0,{&quot;name&quot;:[0,&quot;Bas Westerbaan&quot;],&quot;slug&quot;:[0,&quot;bas&quot;],&quot;bio&quot;:[0,null],&quot;profile_image&quot;:[0,&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/4KeBG8XO1ADZHuEV9v5Hwz/6c90a96cfb1373b208bffc35f3fd71f4/bas.png&quot;],&quot;location&quot;:[0,&quot;The Netherlands&quot;],&quot;website&quot;:[0,&quot;https://bas.westerbaan.name&quot;],&quot;twitter&quot;:[0,&quot;@bwesterb&quot;],&quot;facebook&quot;:[0,null],&quot;publiclyIndex&quot;:[0,true]}]]],&quot;meta_description&quot;:[0,&quot;Recent advances in quantum hardware and software have accelerated the timeline on which quantum attack might happen. By combining graph neural networks and LLMs, we&#39;ve reduced false positives by up to 200x while catching sophisticated zero-day exploits.&quot;],&quot;featured&quot;:[0,false],&quot;html&quot;:[0,&quot;&lt;p&gt;Client-side skimming attacks have a boring superpower: they can steal data without breaking anything. The page still loads. Checkout still completes. All it needs is just one malicious script tag.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;If that sounds abstract, here are two recent examples of such skimming attacks:&lt;/p&gt;&lt;ul&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;In January 2026, &lt;a href=\&quot;https://sansec.io/research/keylogger-major-us-bank-employees\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Sansec reported&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; a browser-side keylogger running on an employee merchandise store for a major U.S. bank, harvesting personal data, login credentials, and credit card information.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;In September 2025, attackers published malicious releases of &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/how-cloudflares-client-side-security-made-the-npm-supply-chain-attack-a-non/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;widely used npm packages&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. If those packages were bundled into front-end code, end users could be exposed to crypto-stealing in the browser.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;/ul&gt;&lt;p&gt;To further our goal of building a better Internet, Cloudflare established a core tenet during our &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.cloudflare.com/innovation-week/birthday-week-2025/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Birthday Week 2025&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;: powerful security features should be accessible &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/enterprise-grade-features-for-all/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;without requiring a sales engagement&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. In pursuit of this objective, we are announcing two key changes today:&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;First, Cloudflare &lt;b&gt;Client-Side Security Advanced&lt;/b&gt; (formerly &lt;b&gt;Page Shield add-on&lt;/b&gt;) is now &lt;a href=\&quot;https://dash.cloudflare.com/?to=/:account/:zone/security/settings?tabs=client-side-abuse\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;available to self-serve&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; customers. And second, domain-based threat intelligence is now complimentary for all customers on the &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/page-shield/#availability\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;free &lt;/u&gt;&lt;b&gt;&lt;u&gt;Client-Side Security&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/b&gt;&lt;u&gt; bundle&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;In this post, weâll explain how this product works and highlight a new AI detection system designed to identify malicious JavaScript while minimizing false alarms. Weâll also discuss several real-world applications for these tools.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;how-cloudflare-client-side-security-works\&quot;&gt;How Cloudflare Client-Side Security works&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#how-cloudflare-client-side-security-works\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;Cloudflare Client-Side Security assesses 3.5 billion scripts per day, protecting 2,200 scripts per enterprise zone on average.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Under the hood, Client-Side Security collects these signals using browser reporting (for example, &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/HTTP/Guides/CSP\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Content Security Policy&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;), which means you donât need scanners or app instrumentation to get started, and there is zero latency impact to your web applications. The only prerequisite is that your traffic is proxied through Cloudflare.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Client-Side Security &lt;b&gt;Advanced&lt;/b&gt; provides immediate access to powerful security features:&lt;/p&gt;&lt;ul&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Smarter malicious script detection:&lt;/b&gt; Using in-house machine learning, this capability is now enhanced with assessments from a Large Language Model (LLM). Read more details below.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Code change monitoring:&lt;/b&gt; Continuous code change detection and monitoring is included, which is essential for meeting compliance like&lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/page-shield/reference/pci-dss/\&quot;&gt; &lt;u&gt;PCI DSS v4&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, requirement 11.6.1.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Proactive blocking rules:&lt;/b&gt; Benefit from positive content security rules that are maintained and enforced through continuous monitoring.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;/ul&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;detecting-malicious-intent-javascripts\&quot;&gt;Detecting malicious intent JavaScripts&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#detecting-malicious-intent-javascripts\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;Managing client-side security is a massive data problem. For an average enterprise zone, our systems observe approximately 2,200 unique scripts; smaller business zones frequently handle around 1,000. This volume alone is difficult to manage, but the real challenge is the volatility of the code.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Roughly a third of these scripts undergo code updates within any 30-day window. If a security team attempted to manually approve every new DOM (document object model) interaction or outbound connection, the resulting overhead would paralyze the development pipeline.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Instead, our detection strategy focuses on &lt;i&gt;what a script is trying to do&lt;/i&gt;. That includes intent classification work &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/how-we-train-ai-to-uncover-malicious-javascript-intent-and-make-web-surfing-safer/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;weâve written about previously&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. In short, we analyze the script&amp;#39;s behavior using an Abstract Syntax Tree (AST). By breaking the code down into its logical structure, we can identify patterns that signal malicious intent, regardless of how the code is obfuscated.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;the-high-cost-of-false-positives\&quot;&gt;The high cost of false positives&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#the-high-cost-of-false-positives\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;Client-side security operates differently than active vulnerability scanners deployed across the web, where a Web Application Firewall (WAF) would constantly observe matched attack signatures. While a WAF constantly blocks high-volume automated attacks, a client-side compromise (such as a breach of an origin server or a third-party vendor) is a rare, high-impact event. In an enterprise environment with rigorous vendor reviews and code scanning, these attacks are rare.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;This rarity creates a problem. Because real attacks are infrequent, a security systemâs detections are statistically more likely to be false positives. For a security team, these false alarms create fatigue and hide real threats. To solve this, we integrated a Large Language Model (LLM) into our detection pipeline, drastically reducing the false positive rate.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;adding-an-llm-based-second-opinion-for-triage\&quot;&gt;Adding an LLM-based second opinion for triage&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#adding-an-llm-based-second-opinion-for-triage\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;Our &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/how-we-train-ai-to-uncover-malicious-javascript-intent-and-make-web-surfing-safer/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;frontline detection engine&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; is a Graph Neural Network (GNN). GNNs are particularly well-suited for this task: they operate on the Abstract Syntax Tree (AST) of the JavaScript code, learning structural representations that capture execution patterns regardless of variable renaming, minification, or obfuscation. In machine learning terms, the GNN learns an embedding of the codeâs graph structure that generalizes across syntactic variations of the same semantic behavior.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;The GNN is tuned for high recall. We want to catch novel, zero-day threats. Its precision is already remarkably high: less than 0.3% of total analyzed traffic is flagged as a false positive (FP). However, at Cloudflareâs scale of &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/how-cloudflares-client-side-security-made-the-npm-supply-chain-attack-a-non/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;3.5 billion scripts assessed daily&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, even a sub-0.3% FP rate translates to a volume of false alarms that can be disruptive to customers.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;The core issue is a classic class imbalance problem. While we can collect extensive malicious samples, the sheer diversity of benign JavaScript across the web is practically infinite. Heavily obfuscated but perfectly legitimate scripts â like bot challenges, tracking pixels, ad-tech bundles, and minified framework builds â can exhibit structural patterns that overlap with malicious code in the GNNâs learned feature space. As much as we try to cover a huge variety of interesting benign cases, the model simply has not seen enough of this infinite variety during training.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;This is precisely where Large Language Models (LLMs) complement the GNN. LLMs possess a deep semantic understanding of real-world JavaScript practices: they recognize domain-specific idioms, common framework patterns, and can distinguish sketchy-but-innocuous obfuscation from genuinely malicious intent.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Rather than replacing the GNN, we designed a cascading classifier architecture:&lt;/p&gt;&lt;ol&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Every script is first evaluated by the GNN&lt;/b&gt;. If the GNN predicts the script as benign, the detection pipeline terminates immediately. &lt;b&gt;This incurs only the minimal latency of the GNN for the vast majority of traffic, completely bypassing the heavier computation time of the LLM&lt;/b&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;If the GNN flags the script as potentially malicious (above the decision threshold), the script is &lt;b&gt;forwarded to an open-source LLM&lt;/b&gt; hosted on Cloudflare &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/workers-ai/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Workers AI&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; for a second opinion.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;The LLM, provided with a security-specialized prompt context, &lt;b&gt;semantically evaluates the scriptâs intent&lt;/b&gt;. If it determines the script is benign, it overrides the GNNâs verdict.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;/ol&gt;&lt;p&gt;This two-stage design gives us the best of both worlds: the GNNâs high recall for structural malicious patterns, combined with the LLMâs broad semantic understanding to filter out false positives.&lt;/p&gt;\n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/438frLuYPU51j0uhtM5foj/10c53b3b3ccc84b00c754c872ad20492/image3.png\&quot; alt=\&quot;\&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;1999\&quot; height=\&quot;1125\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n          &lt;/figure&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/how-we-train-ai-to-uncover-malicious-javascript-intent-and-make-web-surfing-safer/#training-the-model-to-detect-hidden-malicious-intent\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;As we previously explained&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, our GNN is trained on publicly accessible script URLs, the same scripts any browser would fetch. The LLM inference at runtime runs entirely within Cloudflareâs network via &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/workers-ai/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Workers AI&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; using open-source models (we currently use &lt;code&gt;gpt-oss-120b&lt;/code&gt;).&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;As an additional safety net, every script flagged by the GNN is logged to Cloudflare &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/r2/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;R2&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; for posterior analysis. This allows us to continuously audit whether the LLMâs overrides are correct and catch any edge cases where a true attack might have been inadvertently filtered out. Yes, we dogfood our own storage products for our own ML pipeline.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;The results from our internal evaluations on real production traffic are compelling. Focusing on total analyzed traffic under the JS Integrity threat category, the secondary LLM validation layer reduced false positives by nearly 3x: dropping the already low ~0.3% FP rate down to ~0.1%. When evaluating unique scripts, the impact is even more dramatic: the FP rate plummets a whopping ~200x, from ~1.39% down to just 0.007%.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;At our scale, cutting the overall false positive rate by two-thirds translates to millions fewer false alarms for our customers every single day. Crucially, our True Positive (actual attack) detection capability includes a fallback mechanism:as noted above, we audit the LLMâs overrides to check for possible true attacks that were filtered by the LLM.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Because the LLM acts as a highly reliable precision filter in this pipeline, we can now afford to lower the GNNâs decision threshold, making it even more aggressive. This means we catch novel, highly obfuscated True Attacks that would have previously fallen just below the detection boundary, all without overwhelming customers with false alarms. In the next phase, we plan to push this even further.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;catching-zero-days-in-the-wild-the-core-js-router-exploit\&quot;&gt;Catching zero-days in the wild: The &lt;code&gt;core.js&lt;/code&gt; router exploit&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#catching-zero-days-in-the-wild-the-core-js-router-exploit\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;This two-stage architecture is already proving its worth in the wild. Just recently, our detection pipeline flagged a novel, highly obfuscated malicious script (&lt;code&gt;core.js&lt;/code&gt;) targeting users in specific regions.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;In this case, the payload was engineered to commandeer home routers (specifically Xiaomi OpenWrt-based devices). Upon closer inspection via deobfuscation, the script demonstrated significant situational awareness: it queries the router&amp;#39;s WAN configuration (dynamically adapting its payload using parameters like &lt;code&gt;wanType=dhcp&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;wanType=static&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;wanType=pppoe&lt;/code&gt;), overwrites the DNS settings to hijack traffic through Chinese public DNS servers, and even attempts to lock out the legitimate owner by silently changing the admin password. Instead of compromising a website directly, it had been injected into users&amp;#39; sessions via compromised browser extensions.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;To evade detection, the script&amp;#39;s core logic was heavily minified and packed using an array string obfuscator â a classic trick, but effective enough that traditional threat intelligence platforms like VirusTotal have not yet reported detections at the time of this writing.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Our GNN successfully revealed&lt;/b&gt; the underlying malicious structure despite the obfuscation, and the &lt;b&gt;Workers AI LLM confidently confirmed&lt;/b&gt; the intent. Here is a glimpse of the payload showing the target router API and the attempt to inject a rogue DNS server:&lt;/p&gt;\n            &lt;pre class=\&quot;language-JavaScript\&quot;&gt;&lt;code            &lt;p&gt;This is exactly the kind of sophisticated, zero-day threat that a static signature-based WAF would miss but our structural and semantic AI approach catches.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h4 id=\&quot;indicators-of-compromise-iocs\&quot;&gt;Indicators of Compromise (IOCs)&lt;/h4&gt;\n      &lt;a &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;domain-based-threat-intelligence-free-for-all\&quot;&gt;Domain-based threat intelligence free for all&lt;/h2&gt;\n We&amp;#39;ve spent years empowering Cloudflare customers to protect their applications from automated attacks, but the threat landscape has evolved. The industrialization of hybrid automated-and-human abuse presents a complex security challenge to website owners. Consider, for instance, a single account thatâs accessed from New York, London, and San Francisco in the same five minutes. The core question in this case is not âIs this automated?â but rather âIs this authentic?âÂ &lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Website owners need the tools to stop abuse on their website, no matter who itâs coming from&lt;/b&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;During our Birthday Week in 2024, we gifted &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/waf/detections/leaked-credentials/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;leaked credentials detection&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; to all customers, including everyone on a Free plan. Since then, we&amp;#39;ve added &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/bots/additional-configurations/detection-ids/#account-takeover-detections\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;account takeover detection IDs&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; as part of our &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.cloudflare.com/application-services/products/bot-management/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;bot management solution&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; to help identify bots attacking your login pages.Â &lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Now, weâre combining these powerful tools with new ones. &lt;b&gt;Disposable email check&lt;/b&gt; and &lt;b&gt;email risk &lt;/b&gt;help you enforce security preferences for users who sign up with throwaway email addresses, a common tactic for fake account creation and promotion abuse, or whose emails are deemed risky based on email patterns and infrastructure. Weâre also thrilled to introduce &lt;b&gt;Hashed User IDs&lt;/b&gt; â per-domain identifiers generated by cryptographically hashing usernames â that give customers better insight into suspicious account activity and greater ability to mitigate potentially fraudulent traffic, without compromising end user privacy.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;The new capabilities weâre announcing today go beyond automation, identifying abusive behavior and risky identities among human users &lt;/b&gt;&lt;b&gt;&lt;i&gt;and&lt;/i&gt;&lt;/b&gt;&lt;b&gt; bots. &lt;/b&gt;&lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/bots/account-abuse-protection/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Account Abuse Protection&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; is available in Early Access, and any Bot Management Enterprise customer can use these features at no additional cost for a limited period, until the general availability of Cloudflare Fraud Prevention later this year.
